INFLATION, CENTRAL BANKS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL
13
Inflation containment: Cost of
capital, not the policy rate, is doing
the heavy lifting
In the post–global financial crisis period, unprecedented monetary
policy (zero and negative interest rates and quantitative easing)
coupled with robust forward guidance from the Fed anchored
sovereign rates and compressed credit spreads and equity risk
premiums. In other words, capital had an “artificial” price. Today,

after substantial interest rate hikes, quantitative tightening and
the discontinuation of forward guidance, the price of capital is
more real.
Investors have as much to do with that change as central bankers.
Even before September, when the Fed raised its policy rate 25
basis points (bps), yields were already on the rise. Year to date the
10-year U.S. Treasury has risen roughly 75bps to around 5%. Fifty
of those basis points arrived in September alone, placing the move

in the top decile of monthly increases since 1962. Credit spreads
have widened modestly, investment grade (IG) by around 10bps,
high yield by roughly 40bps.
Importantly, markets are expecting more than two additional
hikes in the current cycle (the next one in December). That means
a higher cost of capital already constrains growth and inflation,
even absent an aggressive Fed.
What are the risks to our view?
What might compel the Fed to kick off an aggressive hiking cycle?

A stronger-than-expected economy, with wages accelerating
ahead of productivity, could force the Fed’s hand. So too could a
sustained move in 5-year/5-year inflation breakevens above 3%.
Indeed, the trajectory of data center construction is important
for bond yields. Ten-year U.S. Treasury yields peaked as investors
began to question further AI capex in 2027. A reacceleration in AI
infrastructure investment could push yields higher.
If higher diesel prices depress economic demand, it would present

a particular challenge to policymakers. In the United States, the
cost of diesel has risen to nearly $6.30/gallon from ~$3.80 before
the Middle East conflict began. Diesel at $10/gallon for a quarter
could make a meaningful dent in economic activity. The Fed would
then have to choose between hiking into a weakening economy
or risking that inflation expectations move higher. This is just one
example of the potential that the next growth downturn could be

accompanied by stubborn inflation, which would pose a challenge
for more traditional portfolios.
INFLATION, CENTRAL BANKS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL

### Page 14

INFLATION, CENTRAL BANKS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL
Yes, inflation is still a threat
to your capital
Inflation is the foundation for our economic outlook, and we
believe it should also be reflected in the foundation of your
investment plan. Even though we expect inflation to moderate
through 2027, it still poses a key risk to the purchasing power
of your capital.
Many of our clients may also need to use a different benchmark
than policymakers and economists. From 2019 through 2025, the

headline PCE index rose at a ~3.5% annual rate. Over the same
period, based on our own clients’ spending data, average ticket
sizes on luxury goods purchases increased at more than a 6%
rate. Maintaining purchasing power for the goods and services
that matter most to you likely requires a higher hurdle than the
headline inflation index would suggest.

INFLATION, CENTRAL BANKS AND THE COST OF CAPITAL
The investment
implications
We believe that fixed income markets are
compensating investors appropriately to take on
the risks of sticky inflation and higher policy rates.
Investment grade yields in the United States are
above 6% for the first time since the global financial
crisis. Long-dated municipals yield roughly 5%,
equivalent to an ~8% taxable-equivalent yield.
At the end of the day, bond investing is math, and today’s bond

math is unusually friendly to investors.
At today’s all-in yields, rates would have to rise roughly another 100bps from here
to wipe out a year of IG carry, a worse outcome than even our bear case. Yields may
not have peaked for the cycle. But we believe that investors who add to high grade
credit today can realize solid returns over the medium term even if global central
banks tighten more than we expect and you experience short-term mark to market

losses along the way.

### Page 16

16
Beyond investment grade, preferred equity looks to be well 
positioned to deliver attractive returns to portfolios. In fact, we 
would rather fund U.S. and Canadian banks through preferred 
equity than common equity. Headline yields are around 7% and 
they are a tax efficient asset for U.S.-based investors. Many of the 
catalysts that could drive bank stock outperformance have played 
out. Still, collecting high-single-digit, tax-efficient returns  
by owning bank preferred stock seems attractive from a risk-
reward perspective. 
Private credit may also be a counterintuitive beneficiary of a rising 
rate environment. Consider: In direct lending, realized loan default 
rates range from 1% to 3%, but a broader “shadow” default rate
1

puts loans under stress at ~15% of the business development 
company (BDC) universe.
2
 Investors are compensated for taking 
on this risk: Spreads including discount at issue have widened 
by about 100bps year-to-date. At a ~5% default rate and ~50% 
recovery, loss-adjusted yields on senior-secured direct lending still 
clear ~7.5%.
3

Higher borrowing costs also present opportunity for distressed 
managers who can charge a high price for liquidity, and we are 
seeing new potential in places such as  private credit secondaries 
and asset-backed finance. 
In a world where capital once again has a price, fixed income is 
where that cost is being paid most explicitly to you.

### Page 17

### Page 18

18
We are more positive on core fixed income than we were last year.
Our long-term outlook for traditional multi-asset portfolios that
mix public equities with core fixed income suggests competitive
returns over the next 10-15 years.
However, we see three reasons to add other diversifiers
to portfolios.
First, in large part due to the uncertainty associated with
the potential payback of AI investment, there is a wide range
of possible outcomes.
Options that protect against a sharp decline in equities are trading

at their highest price of the year. Insurance protection against
default for investment grade companies that are borrowing to
finance the AI infrastructure build is approaching levels more
commonly seen in junk issuers. Assets like diversified hedge fund
strategies tend to perform well when volatility is elevated and
could thus help limit portfolio drawdowns. For example, macro
hedge funds have delivered positive performance on average
during quarters when both stocks and bonds are down.

Second, the threat of inflation shocks driven in part by
geopolitical fragmentation should lead to a positive
correlation between stocks and bonds.
That suggests that a traditional 60% stock/40% bond portfolio
is not prepared for a scenario where stocks and bonds are both
falling. Core real assets and commodities respond to inflation
differently than stocks and bonds. Both deserve the consideration
of your long-term capital.
Third, public equity markets have rarely been this

concentrated, largely because of the dominance of AI and
technology more broadly.
The 10 largest stocks in the S&P 500 account for nearly 40%
of the index, the highest share since at least 1980. Of course,
concentration in technology and AI-linked names has driven
strong performance, which we believe will continue.
Still, investors should consider alternative drivers of capital
appreciation. For instance, U.S. core private equity has a 24%
weight to technology and adjacent companies relative to the

S&P 500 at nearly 50%. In exchange for liquidity, private equity
provides the opportunity for capital appreciation without the
concentration of technology. Manager selection is crucial,
though, especially in an environment where software is at risk of
disruption and leverage-driven returns will be harder to generate.
A balanced portfolio
is only the beginning.

### Page 21

21
Sovereigns need capital to defend themselves in a new era of
conflict, where $70,000 drones can be competitive with million-
dollar missile systems, defense inventories are depleted, and the
U.S. security umbrella is not what it once was.
4
Governments will
increasingly need capital to finance their AI ecosystems (“compute
sovereignty” may become a new catchphrase). And sovereigns
certainly need capital for energy security. Conflict in Iran and the

closure of the Strait of Hormuz were first a shock and then
a sobering reality, and the ongoing conflict has only exacerbated
the fragility of global energy supply chains, to say nothing of the
long-term threats from climate-related risks.
Where will the missing capital come from? You will be
asked to share the cost through some mix of tariffs, taxes,
inflation and higher rates.
Global fragmentation should also force an assessment of
geographic diversification. Be thoughtful about which currencies

you save in, which sovereigns you lend to, and consider the global
spectrum of potential beneficiaries.
Which sovereign blocs have
earned your capital?
Global fragmentation should force you to think more critically
about capital allocation across sovereign blocs. Traditional metrics
such as trade balances and policy credibility are useful, but you
must also consider a country’s natural resources and its exposure
to critical bottlenecks. Can a country defend itself? That question

has new resonance.
We suggest assessing sovereign risks against their vulnerability
to four criteria.
• Conflict. Can the sovereign provide its own security, or is
it dependent on outside support at prices that may prove
unsustainable?
• Compute. Does the sovereign have its own AI infrastructure
and research ecosystem? Is it prepared to defend against
AI-enabled cyberattacks?
• Chokepoints. Can the sovereign rely on domestically run
supply chains, or does it depend on foreign suppliers?

• Climate. Is the sovereign vulnerable to the negative impacts of
climate change, and is it positioned to invest in adaptation?
We think this framework can help identify which countries
and regions might be best positioned for the era of global
fragmentation. The bottom line is that all the blocs that we
care about as capital allocators have vulnerabilities to mitigate.
Concentration in one is probably not prudent.
4    Center for Strategic and International Studies. Drone Saturation: Russia’s Shahed

Campaign.  2025; CSIS. The Cost and Value in Air and Missile Defense Intercepts. 2025.

### Page 22

22
22
First, the United States seems relatively well-positioned to
weather geopolitical fragmentation over the long term, but
there are still critical risks to address. Fiscal capacity, resource
endowment and more limited bottleneck vulnerability all argue
in favor of Treasuries and the dollar. But there is a risk in central
banks and reserve managers diversifying their savings away
from the United States. U.S. policymakers and business leaders
are beginning to address competitive vulnerabilities in areas

such as semiconductor manufacturing, skilled engineering labor
and rare earths. The ongoing El Niño could cause disruption in
insurance markets and food prices, and we are waiting to see
the potential response from insurers and policymakers.
Europe: Market outlooks diverge among equity,
sovereign credit and corporate credit.
While bondholders are funding capital requirements into needed
resiliency spending, stockholders can benefit from that spending.

Europe’s challenges include elevated oil and gas prices
(most European nations are not energy self-sufficient), rising
government bond yields, high levels of debt and more frequent
and severe heatwaves and drought. In France, 10-year government
bonds now yield more than their Italian counterparts for the first
time since the creation of the eurozone, a roughly 130bps swing
from early 2024. (At the end of Q2, France’s public debt-to-GDP
ratio was 119% versus 140% for Italy.) French elections in 2027,

which will likely  produce a contest between candidates on the far
left and far right, loom large.

GLOBAL FRAGMENTATION
European policymakers on the national and pan-European level 
have called for greater investment in defense, technology and 
energy security in lieu of expanded welfare programs. We are 
also monitoring the potential for broader adaptation spending to 
mitigate the adverse impacts of a hotter, less predictable climate. 
German defense spending has doubled since 2022, with Germany 
notably harnessing its industrial infrastructure to diversify away 
from automobile manufacturing.
5
 But the broader push for 
investment has encountered political resistance and limitations  
in scaling up capacity. Only about a third of European Union 
counter-drone and short-range air defense programs are expected 
to be complete by 2030.
6

Given the emerging fiscal strains for European sovereigns, 
we prefer to place our capital in European multinational 
corporations over sovereign debt.
We continue to invest in European equity markets, where 
corporate earnings have proven stronger than expected. Capital 
has flowed to Europe’s beneficiaries of global fragmentation. 
The ranks include the dominant Dutch company that prints 
every advanced chip and French and British defense companies 
capturing rearmament orders.   
Asia: Different blocs, different trades
Geopolitical fragmentation plays differently across Asian markets. 
China is in many ways a beneficiary of the trend. It is an industrial 
and manufacturing power, with an enviable supply of energy. The 
country remains a major exporter (its trade surplus continues 
to set records) and it is building a parallel AI ecosystem with 
sophisticated low-cost models. But economic growth remains 
weak and uneven as its property sector continues to struggle and 
domestic consumption recovers slowly. In September, fixed asset 
investment fell 7.2%.
7

A U.S. or European investor who is considering allocating capital 
to China must also weigh the potential for alignment risk—that is, 
the potential for diverging geopolitical interests with China.  
On balance, we opt for a selective and relatively small allocation 
to Chinese markets, but you shouldn’t ignore it entirely. 
Korea, Taiwan and Japan sell what a fragmented world needs, 
e.g., semiconductors, AI infrastructure and shipbuilding 
equipment. Korea holds roughly 70% of global DRAM capacity, 
while Taiwan dominates advanced chip manufacturing.
8
We remain constructive on Korea and Taiwan equities and 
selectively positive on Japan.  
Latin America: Opportunities for capital in 
nearshoring and commodities
Latin America offers exposure to two related opportunities, the 
nearshoring that is accelerating amid global fragmentation and 
a bull market in commodities.
We see Mexico as a clear beneficiary of nearshoring. The United 
States–Mexico–Canada Agreement (USMCA) remains intact, 
despite political rhetoric from the U.S. administration. Mexico’s 
manufacturing base and deep capital market links to the United 
States underscore the country’s investment potential. That 
potential may increase as firms sell higher value-add exports and 
services into U.S. markets. In fact, tech products now make up 
nearly 50% of Mexico’s exports, and the United States receives 
40% of its imported servers from Mexico.
9
 For investors, it’s 
helpful, too, that the peso is a high-carry convertible currency. 
The rest of the region offers investors a commodity-FX trade: 
Chile and Peru sit on roughly a quarter of the world’s copper 
reserves.
10
 Brazil not only adds iron ore, agriculture and oil, but 
also already hosts 218 data centers, with the potential to double 
capacity by 2030.
11
 As investors in the region, we favor risk assets 
over currencies. We still favor Latin America, even after the Brazil 
election surprise, with a special preference for Mexico and a more 
neutral stance on Chile and Peru.

### Page 24

24
The case for gold and commodities 
In a fragmented world, we believe that gold and other commodities may be  
worth considering as additions to portfolios.
Turning first to gold, many countries have expressed a desire to hold savings in  
something outside the dollar-based system and broadly diversify away from fiat 
currencies.  As a result, central banks have increased their gold holdings as a share  
of overall reserves. The shift hasn’t been uniform, but IMF data shows the dollar’s 
share of official foreign exchange reserves has fallen to roughly 57% from a peak  
of 70% in 2000.
12
Commodities such as copper, oil and gas serve as vital components in compute supply 
chains and national resiliency initiatives. Electrification and grid enhancement help drive 
a projected 25% supply shortfall of copper and 40% shortfall of lithium by 2035.
13

We expect supply shortages to persist even as producers ramp up to meet growing 
demand. New copper mines, for example, take more than 16 years to build.
14
 Importantly, 
too, exposure to commodities offers a portfolio hedge to higher inflation. In fact, over  
the last 50 years commodities have delivered positive returns on average in quarters 
when stock and bond prices both decline.
Despite constrained supply and ongoing structural demand, the stocks of commodity  
and natural resource companies are generally less expensive than the overall market.  
The global energy and materials sectors trade at a 33% and 15% discount to the  
S&P 500, respectively, and sport higher dividend yields.

### Page 25

Do security spending beneficiaries
deserve your capital?
Global fragmentation has supported businesses that benefit from
security spending. The trend will continue, we believe. Indeed,
global fragmentation could be one of the best reasons to
invest globally in equity markets. Most countries face security
vulnerabilities they need to address and most markets host the
companies that benefit from the spending, from Japan and Korea
to France, Sweden and Mexico.

Generally, buyers of security-related products and services
care more about reliability and quick delivery than price. As
we’ve discussed, supply shortages of critical components like
transformers (with lead times between 2.5 and 3 years) and
copper will persist for the foreseeable future.
15
That gives security
companies pricing power and order books that run for years.
Strong earnings (seven of every 10 stocks we follow on this theme
have positive earnings revisions over the last 12 months) and

rising expectations have pushed a basket of security stocks to
a nearly 25% return year to date. We expect continued earnings
growth will drive the next leg of outperformance. Active stock
selection will be critical, though, in separating the market’s
winners and losers and identifying the firms that deserve
your capital.
Where we are placing
our capital
We are placing our own capital behind the need for security
spending in a fragmented world. JPMorganChase’s Security

and Resiliency Initiative (SRI) is a USD 1.5 trillion, 10-year
plan to facilitate, finance and invest in industries critical to
economic security and resiliency.
16
The firm will make direct
equity investments for an initial amount of USD 10 billion in select
companies, primarily in the United States but also in Europe,
to help enhance their growth, spur innovation and accelerate
strategic manufacturing. The remainder of the pledge is designed
to secure debt and loan financing for similar initiatives. Some

recent examples include a financing commitment to MP Materials,
a rare earth metals company, and an equity investment in Shield
AI, a leader in defense technology and autonomous aircraft.
We are also focused on the USD 1.3 trillion upgrade needed for
Europe’s electrical grid.
We are committed to doing our part to address the vulnerabilities
that are inherent in a fragmented world.

### Page 27

WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?27
Investors must consider four related issues:
• How far and how fast will AI adoption (early use of AI models)
and diffusion (more extensive use across the economy) progress?
• How many trillions will AI need to deliver to generate returns
for investors?
• Will markets be willing to finance the infrastructure
build-out, and at what price?
• Which layer of the equity stack will be best positioned to generate returns:

the infrastructure layer that builds the AI data centers,
the compute layer that rents out the semiconductors, or the
application layer that turns model tokens (chunks of text)
into durable business revenue?
The AI build-out has created a staggering
demand for capital. Going forward,
political pressure will increasingly
impact the capex boom.
Since 2022, AI capex has totaled over
USD 2 trillion, with trillions more
expected in the next several years.

Will the capex continue?
Yes, if investors like you fund it, and
regulations allow it. And, perhaps more
importantly for long-term investors:
Will AI spending ultimately pay off?
These may be the most important
questions for investors heading into 2027.

### Page 28

28
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?
These four issues inform much of today’s market debate. If you
already own U.S. equities, you have been a passive participant.
That’s because, as our Chairman of Market and Investment
Strategy, Michael Cembalest, has shown, a basket of 54 AI stocks
represents over half of the S&P 500 market cap, 66% of earnings
growth, 59% of the index price return and 82% of capex/R&D
growth since January 2024.
17
We believe in AI’s long-term potential to boost productivity and

corporate profits. But investors are asking serious questions
about the magnitude of the infrastructure build, and markets
may be starting to shift their perception of who the ultimate
winners will be.

### Page 29

WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?29
If AI adoption and diffusion stall, nothing else really matters. The infrastructure  
gets stranded, the compute rents compress and the applications never find their  
potential customers. 
Data tells us that these are still very early days for AI adoption. According to Ramp,  
a financial technology company that tracks business spending, 80% of AI spending is 
driven by 1% of businesses, overwhelmingly technology startups buying tokens from 
other technology startups.
18
 In an analysis of AI and productivity, J.P. Morgan Asset 
Management estimates that only 6% of U.S. work hours are AI-assisted today, even  
as two-thirds of tasks may be theoretically exposed.
19

To justify the trillions of capital targeted for the AI build-out, adoption and diffusion must 
broaden beyond the technology sector and deepen beyond pilot programs. We think this 
will happen, for three reasons.
First, enterprise adoption is shifting from experimentation to productivity. Most large 
enterprises remain skittish about AI agents, which operate with minimal human 
involvement. But as corporate leaders grow more comfortable with the technology, 
we think they will increasingly deploy agentic models that will boost productivity and 
compute intensity per user.
Second, the history of transformative technologies tells us that falling prices unlock 
use cases faster than they compress revenue. Since 2023, the price of a given level 
of AI capability has fallen at a 13x annual rate, which is four times faster than DNA 
sequencing.
20
 Over the same period, annual run-rate revenues for the leading model 
providers have grown from zero to well over USD 100 billion.
21
 Goldman Sachs projects 
a 24x increase in token consumption through 2030 as use cases expand.
22
 J.P. Morgan 
Asset Management projects that overall AI adoption could rise to 40% of today’s tasks by 
2040.
23
Third, consumer agents, which can autonomously handle a range of tasks, could 
gain traction. Meta’s Muse has emerged as one contender in this arena. In many 
large economic sectors like healthcare and transportation, AI models still have a low 
penetration rate, and that too could change.
24

The bear case for AI adoption is straightforward: AI remains a productivity tool for 
technologists and a novelty for everyone else. The error rate remains too high and 
enterprise adoption stalls while most consumers decide to take a pass on the new 
AI spreads through  
the economy—or  
all bets are off

### Page 31

WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?31
AI spending only pays off if the economy generates enough 
revenue from it, and does so quickly enough, to cover the cost of 
the capital deployed (especially data centers). Given the sheer size 
of the current pipeline, and anxiety about how much of it moves 
from plans to completed projects, we expect growing investor 
pressure to see that revenues do in fact materialize.
To understand the challenge, consider what it takes to break even 
or pay for itself year to year, including the cost of investor capital. 
A gigawatt of AI data center capacity costs roughly USD 40 billion. 
To cover its cost of capital, a 1GW facility must generate about 
USD 11 billion in revenue per year.
26
  The ~129GW of global frontier 
data center capacity expected by 2029 would therefore need to 
generate about USD 1.5 trillion a year in compute revenue, largely 
sourced from the application companies that must rent computing 
power to produce products and services that are consumed by 
enterprises, consumers and governments. These companies, 
in particular the private model labs, likely need to earn 50% 
margins above their computing costs. That means end-customer 
AI spending would need to total roughly twice that amount, about 
USD 3 trillion per year. 
So how much revenue could the AI ecosystem earn in the early 
2030s? Under reasonable assumptions, we estimate:
• Enterprises could spend about USD 2.5 trillion a year on AI 
through employee AI tools and agents.
• Consumer sectors could add about USD 750 billion in revenue 
through advertising and subscriptions.
• Governments could add another USD 400 billion.
27

That gets AI revenue to about USD ~3.5 trillion, about USD 700 
billion above the breakeven rate needed for the data centers to 
pay for themselves year to year. That surplus represents returns 
for investors in the compute and application layer. It may seem 
sizable, but it is based on an addressable market that is merely an 
estimate and far from assured.
A closely watched risk is whether a large share of AI-driven 
revenue, especially from large enterprises, comes from job 
displacement—replacing workers, rather than creating new 
How many trillions  
does AI need to deliver? 
demand. If half of potential enterprise AI spending replaces roles 
that would otherwise be filled, and AI costs about half as much 
as the worker it replaces, AI would absorb roughly 24 million 
knowledge worker jobs worldwide over five years, or about 1.3% 
of the global knowledge workforce each year. That is disruptive, 
but it is about a third of the pace at which U.S. manufacturing shed 
its workforce in the 2000s (~3.4% a year) amid factory automation 
and China’s entry into the World Trade Organization. Much of the 
AI adjustment could come through roles left unfilled rather than 
layoffs. The bigger risk is concentration: Displacement that falls 
heavily on specific occupations or regions could test political limits 
well before the aggregate numbers do.
28

To illustrate, if two-thirds of those displaced roles were in the 
United States, the U.S. would absorb roughly 16 million workers 
over five years, or about 3 million a year. That is roughly 2% of 
payrolls annually, more than four times the pace of the 2000s 
manufacturing decline. It would approach the rate of job loss 
during the Great Recession, only sustained for five years rather 
than two. Even if most of AI job displacement avoids layoffs, it 
would at the very least eliminate  the economy’s normal annual 
job growth. As the midterm election results suggest, it seems very 
unlikely that there would be political tolerance of this outcome. 
J.P. Morgan Asset Management’s analysis of AI’s impact on the 
economy suggests that job disruption is more likely to occur within 
jobs rather than eliminate jobs outright. After all, every job is 
ultimately a collection of tasks, and to date, AI can automate or 
augment only a portion of them. Moreover, while we anticipate AI 
diffusion across the economy will be profound, it will take time, 
and adoption is expected to be uneven and gradual. In our view,

this steady uptake should help limit the risk of widespread
job displacement.
Turning back to the economics of the AI build-out: The gap
between the revenue required for the AI ecosystem to break even

and the potential revenue from sources such as enterprises and
consumers is a critical variable. When the potential market is far
larger than what the AI ecosystem needs to break even, every new
data center looks like a solid investment. But as the infrastructure
pipeline grows toward the size of the addressable AI market, the
gap shrinks, and so do the incentives to keep building.
We believe the infrastructure pipeline is entering a more
selective phase. First, the addressable market is still very much

debated, especially after frontier model lab revenues decelerated
recently. Second, bond markets have started to balk at excessive
leverage and aggressive infrastructure plans from smaller AI
players. Finally, overbuilding is often how capital investment
booms end. The railroads and the internet were both tremendously
productive innovations. But far too much track and fiber were laid
before demand arrived. As a result, capital markets suffered severe

losses. In the AI cycle, though, more restrictions around data center
development reduce the risk that capacity exceeds demand.
Going forward, companies will find it difficult to convince
investors that they need additional data center capacity beyond
the current pipeline especially in light of the likelihood of a
more onerous regulatory environment. Therefore, value may
increasingly accrue to companies that control the crucial
bottlenecks, or have the pricing power to work through constraints

such as political pressure, regulatory shifts, power availability, grid
access, permitting speed and financing.

### Page 33

33
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?33
As even casual observers of the AI story understand, financing will be key to
how the plot unfolds. As a result, before you underwrite the equity at any layer
of the AI ecosystem, you should be comfortable with the debt on a company’s
balance sheet. (Another lesson from Economics 101: In a debt default, equity
value disappears.)
Some AI companies have recently hit the limits of what public debt markets

will tolerate. Consider the example of unsecured bonds from CoreWeave,
a “neocloud” (typically highly leveraged providers that rent out their compute
capacity). Their bonds trade at distressed level spreads well over 1,000bps.
Does this signal the beginning of the end of the AI boom?  No, but it does
suggest a different opportunity set within public and private markets.
Although AI debt is growing (AI-related issuance will top USD 300 billion this

year), debt growth as a share of GDP is still much lower than previous historical
leverage cycles and more political hurdles to data center development could
also result in a slower pace of leverage growth.

### Page 34

WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?
Today, most of the AI debt is issued by investment grade borrowers with some of the
strongest balance sheets in the world. The hyperscalers as a whole (Alphabet, Amazon,
Microsoft, Oracle) carry interest coverage above 50x, compared with ~9x for the
median investment-grade issuer. Oracle is clearly an outlier. We still see opportunity in
some investment grade hyperscaler debt.
New financing structures have drawn scrutiny, though, because they can obscure who

owns the debt and what collateral sits behind it. Case in point: ~USD 165 billion in off-
balance-sheet from Nvidia support roughly 6.5GW of planned compute capacity from new
model providers.
29
Nvidia aims to cultivate a customer base beyond the hyperscalers and
frontier model labs. In principle, this should reduce concentration risk
in the AI ecosystem. But off-balance-sheet structures add to the opacity of the system
and are likely raising the cost of capital.

Artificial intelligence has shifted the technology sector from an asset-light to an asset-
heavy business model. A decade ago, the largest cloud companies reinvested about 7%
of their revenue in capital spending. This year, analysts’ consensus expects roughly 36%.
That is nearly the reinvestment rate of a regulated utility, and it absorbs almost all of the
cloud companies’ operating cash flow.
The further you move from the hyperscalers, the more asset-heavy the business model

becomes. Neoclouds such as CoreWeave spend close to USD 3 in capex for every dollar
of revenue, and they fund the difference with debt. The demand for capital is also growing
in the power sector, where U.S. utilities plan nearly USD 1.5 trillion of investment over the
next five years.
30
Today the assets in focus are GPUs and data centers. Tomorrow they could be
autonomous vehicles and robots. Either way, asset-heavy models need financing. We have

been exploring opportunities to provide that financing through private markets to data
center developers and neoclouds that are struggling to raise capital in public markets on
attractive terms. This is a riskier proposition, which comes with commensurately higher
potential returns.
29    Semianalysis. Nvidia’s Backstop Universe – Heads I Win, Tails Who Loses? September 11, 2026.
30    Edison Electric Institute. EEI Data: Electric Companies to Invest $1.4 Trillion. 2026.

### Page 35

35
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?35
What you must believe to own
the infrastructure layer
The infrastructure layer (semiconductors, memory, electrical
equipment, power generation and construction) gets paid on
delivery. Its revenue is a function of gigawatts built.
To allocate your capital to AI infrastructure firms, you need to
believe the build-out will be larger than what investors have priced
into the stocks. That has so far proved true. Wall Street analysts

have underestimated hyperscaler capex growth by an average of
50 percentage points in each of the last three years.
However, as we have explained, we believe we are reaching the
limits of upward revisions to the infrastructure layer, even if
demand for compute seems to be outstripping physical capacity.
To believe that the equity of the infrastructure layer can
outperform from here, you also probably need to believe that
the political restrictions on data center development are less

restrictive than what is currently priced into markets. At the time
of writing, semiconductor stocks were already pricing in between
a 20% and 40% decline in earnings. Further, an ever-larger fleet
of AI data centers would require consistent replenishment of
semiconductors to support revenues. Finally, sovereign demand
for AI capacity may result in a global data center fleet built for
redundancy and security, not capital efficiency.
Our base case scenario assumes decelerating capex growth,

but that comes with a silver lining. A slower build preserves
scarcity, and scarcity may be more profitable for the rest of the AI
ecosystem, at least in the near term. Limiting data center supply
could help maintain pricing power for both computing power and
intelligence itself, even if it limits the potential for AI disruption.
Focus on the
hyperscaler elite
We are more bullish about the prospects for hyperscalers. Looking
across the public market AI ecosystem, we think the group may be

most deserving of your capital today.
The hyperscalers could benefit whether compute is abundant or
scarce, cheap or expensive. They draw strength from the reach of
their businesses: They own the data centers, run the clouds and
sell the AI applications (search, advertising, productivity software
and consumer products). When compute is scarce, they benefit by
charging higher rents for their capacity. When compute is more
plentiful, and thus less costly, hyperscalers’  own application

businesses benefit from lower prices.
We can also see a clear path to a return on their AI investments.
Sales growth in core cloud businesses is accelerating. Meta’s and
Alphabet’s investments in AI-driven advertising  are starting to pay
off. Hyperscaler balance sheets could bend, but we do not believe
they will break. Recent disappointing earnings reports are also
providing an attractive entry point for investors.
A moderating pace of infrastructure build, which we now expect,

would also ease pressure on hyperscalers’ free cash flow. Finally,
the hyperscalers now trade at attractive prices in both the debt
and equity markets. As an aggregate, their stocks trade at a very
slight P/E premium to the market (well below their own long
run averages) and price-to-earnings growth ratios suggest that
markets aren’t giving them very much credit for continued growth.
Heading into 2027, we believe hyperscalers present the best
risk-adjusted returns for your public equity capital.

### Page 37

37
WILL THE AI CAPEX BOOM ULTIMATELY PAY OFF?37
The application layer sits at the top of the AI stack: the frontier 
labs, enterprise AI platforms and vertical software companies 
that turn tokens into products customers buy. Companies in the 
application layer operate in a variety of sectors, from coding 
and customer service to healthcare and law. Their revenue is a 
function of tokens consumed or, increasingly, outcomes delivered. 
The application layer’s main cost is the compute it rents.
Frontier labs and other application firms sell “intelligence” (the 
ability to reason and work toward a goal) for which they can now 
charge a premium price. Today, frontier labs in the application 
layer earn 60%–90% gross margins.
31
Markets consistently question whether the frontier labs’ 
exceptional profit margins are sustainable. These labs could face 
growing competition from China-origin open-weight models, which 
now account for ~80% of tokens generated on platforms such as 
OpenRouter, an AI platform/service provider. Enterprises are also 
keen to limit the cost of their employees’ token usage, which could 
impinge on frontier lab revenue.
32

To be bullish about the application layer, you need to believe in 
some combination of the following scenarios: 
•  Model quality matters. Customers don’t choose models 
on price alone. They weigh cost against quality, reliability, 
latency (essentially how long it takes for a model to respond 
to a query) and how well a model aligns with a customer’s 
workflows. Open-weight models will certainly improve, but 
we think frontier models will command a premium for the 
foreseeable future. Indeed, they still account for over 90%  
of enterprise token spending. 
• Pricing could move from tokens to outcomes.  
Under usage-based pricing, revenue is tied to a unit that 
should keep getting cheaper. Under outcome-based pricing, 
revenue is tied to the value of the work. As a result, falling 
token costs widen margins without reducing revenue. 
• Demand extends meaningfully beyond today’s base. Enterprise 
adoption of agentic workflows, household adoption of 
consumer agents and physical world AI applications such 
as robots begin to take hold. AI’s promised future starts to 
become real.
The history of value creation favors the “second wave” companies 
in a capex boom, the firms that build on the early infrastructure. 
That is perhaps the most persuasive argument for the value of the 
application layer. The rise of railroads in the United States enabled 
the launch of Sears department stores. Electricity set the stage 
for Hollywood entertainment. The internet enabled e-commerce 
and the app store. Another lesson from history is that innovation 
cycles lead to increased demand in other sectors of the economy. 
Today, we believe that demand for experiences, travel, luxury,  
wellness, longevity and sports could all increase as AI diffuses. 
At the beginning of the electricity and computer era, public 
markets reflected the incumbents. But within 30 years, nearly half 
of the 10 largest publicly traded stocks in the market were native 
to those technologies. We believe the same pattern will prevail 
in the AI era. Consider exposure in today’s private markets to the 
potential public giants of tomorrow.
What you must believe 
to invest your capital in  
the application layer

2027 ANNUAL OUTLOOK
Conclusion
A focus on your capital—demand for capital, cost of capital—will reverberate
through markets in the coming year. Do you believe in the AI boom enough to
provide the financing that could bridge the gap between capital investment
and durable revenue? Will you bet on the beneficiaries of security spending?
Will you use the potential returns from higher interest rates to lower risk
within your portfolio while maintaining your previous return target? Cash may

even be a slightly more attractive proposition.
As always, you’ll want to build a portfolio that is sturdy enough, and flexible
enough, to help you achieve your long-term goals. We are here to help assess
the investment potential and challenges across the financial landscape.
In 2027 and beyond, we aim to help ensure you charge the right price
for your capital.

Indices are shown for illustrative purposes only. An index
is unmanaged, is not an investment product and may not

5-year / 5-year forward inflation breakeven rate (5y5y): A
market-based measure of expected average inflation over the
five-year period beginning five years from today, derived from
nominal and inflation-protected government bond yields.
Agentic AI / agentic models: Artificial intelligence systems
designed to plan and carry out multi-step tasks with
limited human intervention, often by using tools, retrieving
information and taking actions within defined workflows.

AI application layer: The part of the AI ecosystem
that converts models and tokens into products or
services customers buy, including frontier labs,
enterprise platforms, agents and vertical software.
AI compute layer: The part of the AI ecosystem that supplies and
rents the computing capacity used to train and run AI models,
including chips, servers, cloud infrastructure and related services.
AI infrastructure layer: The physical foundation
of the AI ecosystem, including semiconductors,

memory, networking, electrical equipment, power
generation, data centers and construction.
Alignment risk: The investment risk that a country,
company or asset becomes disadvantaged as geopolitical
interests, standards, trade relationships or financial
systems diverge across competing blocs.
Alternative assets: Investment categories outside
traditional public equities and core fixed income,
including private equity, private credit, hedge funds,
infrastructure, real estate and commodities.

Basis point (bp or bps): One one-hundredth of a percentage
point. For example, 100 basis points equal 1 percentage point.
Bottleneck basket: A thematic group of companies that
make or control scarce physical inputs needed for economic
and national security, such as semiconductors, commodities,
power generation equipment and gas turbines.
BRIC countries: Brazil, Russia, India and China,
a grouping of large emerging economies.
Business development company (BDC): A publicly traded or non-

traded investment company that primarily lends to or invests in
small and middle-market businesses, often through private credit.
Capital expenditure (capex): Spending to acquire, build or
upgrade long-lived assets such as data centers, machinery,
power infrastructure and other property or equipment.
Carry: The income earned from holding an investment,
such as a bond coupon or credit spread, before
changes in market price and other costs.
Central bank policy rate: The short-term interest rate

set or targeted by a central bank to influence financial
conditions, economic activity and inflation.
Consumer Price Index (CPI): A measure of inflation that
tracks changes over time in prices paid by consumers
for a representative basket of goods and services.
Correlation: A statistical measure of how two variables
move in relation to one another. A correlation of 1 indicates
they move together, 0 indicates no consistent relationship
and -1 indicates they move in opposite directions.

Credit spread: The additional yield on a bond or loan relative
to a comparable government security or benchmark rate,
compensating investors for credit and liquidity risk.
Debt-to-GDP ratio: A measure of a country’s public debt
relative to its gross domestic product, commonly used to assess
the scale and sustainability of government indebtedness.
Developed markets (DM): Countries with advanced economies,
established financial markets and generally high income levels,

such as the United States, Japan and many European economies.
Direct lending: Private credit provided directly by non-
bank lenders to companies, often through negotiated
senior-secured loans to middle-market borrowers.

### Page 44

Diversified hedge funds: Pooled investment strategies
that use multiple approaches, such as macro, relative value,
long/short and event-driven investing, to seek returns with
lower dependence on broad stock and bond markets.
Emerging markets (EM): Countries with developing
economies and financial markets that may offer higher
growth potential but generally involve greater economic,
political, currency and market risk than developed markets.
Federal funds rate: The interest rate at which U.S. banks lend

reserve balances to one another overnight; the Federal Reserve
sets a target range for this rate as its principal policy tool.
Free cash flow: Cash generated by a company after operating
expenses and capital expenditures, available for debt repayment,
dividends, share repurchases, acquisitions or reinvestment.
Frontier labs / model providers: Organizations that develop
and commercialize leading-edge artificial intelligence models,
often selling access through subscriptions, application

programming interfaces or usage-based pricing.
Gross margin: Revenue less the direct cost of providing
goods or services, expressed as a percentage of revenue.
High yield: Corporate debt rated below investment
grade, generally offering higher yields to compensate
investors for greater credit and default risk.
Hyperscalers: Large cloud-computing providers able to
deliver computing resources at a scale and speed that
supports major enterprises and internet services.

Inflation breakeven rate: The difference between the yields
of nominal and inflation-protected government bonds of
similar maturity, commonly interpreted as a market-based
measure of expected inflation plus an inflation risk premium.
Infrastructure: Long-lived physical assets and
systems that support economic activity, including
power generation and transmission, transportation,
communications and data center assets.
Interest coverage ratio: A measure of a borrower’s ability

to meet interest payments, typically calculated as earnings
before interest and taxes divided by interest expense.
Investment grade (IG): Bonds or borrowers rated at
or above the minimum investment grade threshold by
recognized credit-rating agencies, indicating comparatively
lower expected default risk than high yield debt.
Latency: The time required for an artificial intelligence
model or computing system to respond to a request.
Liquidity: The ease with which an asset can be bought or

sold without materially affecting its price. Private investments
generally offer less liquidity than publicly traded securities.
Loss-adjusted yield: An investment’s yield after
deducting expected credit losses, typically based on
assumptions for default rates and recovery values.
Nearshoring: Relocating production or services closer to a
company’s end market, often to neighboring or regionally aligned
countries, to reduce supply-chain risk and transportation costs.

Neocloud: A specialized cloud provider, often focused on
renting AI computing capacity and frequently relying more
heavily on external financing than established hyperscalers.
Nominal GDP: The value of goods and services
produced by an economy measured at current
prices, without adjusting for inflation.
Off-balance-sheet commitment: A contractual
obligation or financial exposure that is not recorded as
conventional debt on the sponsoring company’s balance

sheet, though it may still create economic risk.
Open-source model: An artificial intelligence model whose
underlying code, model weights or technical components are made
available for others to use, modify or deploy, subject to its license.
Outcome-based pricing: A pricing model in which customers
pay for the value or result delivered, rather than for the
number of users, software seats or tokens consumed.
Overnight index swap / overnight rate futures: Market

instruments used to express expectations for the future path of
short-term interest rates linked to an overnight benchmark.

DEFINITIONS OF INDICES AND TERMS
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price
index: A broad measure of prices paid by U.S. consumers
for goods and services. The Federal Reserve uses the
PCE price index as its preferred inflation gauge.
Price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple: A valuation measure calculated
by dividing a company’s share price by its earnings per share.
Private credit: Privately negotiated lending by non-bank
investors to companies, typically offering less liquidity than

public bonds and often using floating interest rates.
Real assets: Investments tied to physical assets or resources,
including infrastructure, real estate and commodities, that
may provide income and potential sensitivity to inflation.
Real return: An investment return after adjusting for inflation.
Recovery rate: The share of principal and accrued value
that lenders recover after a borrower defaults.
Reserve currency: A currency widely held by
central banks and institutions and commonly used

in international trade, finance and payments.
S&P 500 Index: A market-capitalization-weighted
index of 500 leading U.S. companies, widely used as
a gauge of the U.S. large-cap equity market.
Security and Resiliency Initiative (SRI): JPMorganChase’s
USD 1.5 trillion, 10-year initiative to facilitate, finance
and invest in industries considered critical to economic
security and resiliency, including an initial USD 10
billion of direct investments in select companies.

Secured debt: Borrowing backed by specified collateral
that lenders may claim if the borrower fails to repay.
Secured Overnight Financing Rate (SOFR): A broad
measure of the cost of borrowing cash overnight
collateralized by U.S. Treasury securities and a widely
used reference rate for U.S. dollar financing.
Shadow default rate: A broader measure of credit stress
that goes beyond reported payment defaults to include
indicators such as non-accruals, distressed payment-

in-kind arrangements, material maturity extensions,
foreclosures and deeply discounted loan marks.
Sovereign credit: Debt issued or guaranteed by
a national government, whose risk reflects the
sovereign’s fiscal position, economic capacity, policy
credibility and ability and willingness to repay.
Take-or-pay contract: An agreement requiring a customer
to pay for a minimum amount of capacity, goods or
services whether or not the customer uses them.

Taxable-equivalent yield: The pretax yield a taxable bond
would need to provide to match the after-tax yield of a tax-
exempt bond, based on an investor’s applicable tax rate.
Token: A basic unit of text or data processed by an artificial
intelligence model. Model usage and pricing are often measured
by the number of input, output and reasoning tokens consumed.
U.S. Treasury yield: The market interest rate on
debt issued by the U.S. government. Treasury yields

serve as benchmarks for borrowing costs and
investor expectations across financial markets.
Unsecured debt: Borrowing that is not backed by specific
collateral, leaving lenders dependent on the issuer’s
general creditworthiness and claim on its assets.
USMCA: The United States-Mexico-Canada Agreement,
the trade agreement governing economic relations
among the three countries and replacing the
North American Free Trade Agreement.
Usage-based pricing: A pricing model in which customer

charges rise or fall with measured consumption, such as tokens
processed, computing capacity used or transactions completed.
Vertical software: Software designed for the workflows
and specialized requirements of a specific industry or
profession, such as healthcare, law or financial services.

### Page 47

UNDERSTANDING LONG-TERM ESTIMATES
Our investment management research incorporates our
proprietary projections of the returns and volatility of each asset
class over the long term, as well as estimates of the correlations
among asset classes. Clearly, financial firms cannot predict how
markets will perform in the future. But we do believe that by
analyzing current economic and market conditions and historical
market trends, and then, most critically, making projections

of future economic growth, inflation and real yields for each
country, we can estimate the long-term performance for an entire
asset class, given current and our estimated equilibrium levels.
The “equilibrium” level shows the average or central tendency
of a market or macroeconomic variable such as yield or credit
spread that is expected to prevail over the long term, because
the level represents the value inherent in a given market. The
return assumptions are based on our proprietary process of

using a building block approach for each of the asset classes. For
instance, the building blocks for equity consist of our projections
on inflation, real earnings growth, dividend yield and the impact
of valuations. The building blocks for fixed income consist of
our projections for future yields and the change in bond prices.
The estimates for alternatives are driven by our historical
analysis and judgment about the relationship to public markets.

It is possible—indeed, probable—that actual returns will vary
considerably from this expectation, even for a number of years.
But we believe that market returns will always at some point
return to the equilibrium trend. We further believe that these
kinds of forward-looking assessments are far more accurate
than historical trends in deciding what asset class performance
will be, and how best to determine an optimal asset mix.
In reviewing this material, please understand that all references

to expected return are not promises, or even estimates, of actual
returns one may achieve. The assumptions are not based on
specific products and do not reflect fees, such as investment
management fees, oversight fees, transaction costs or other
expenses that could reduce return. They simply show what the
long-term return should be, according to our best estimates
of current and equilibrium conditions. Also note that actual
performance may be affected by the expertise of the person who

actually manages these investments, both in picking individual
securities and possibly adjusting the mix periodically to take
advantage of asset class undervaluations and overvaluations
caused by market trends. For the purpose of this analysis volatility
is defined as a statistical measure of the dispersion of return for
a given allocation and is measured as the standard deviation of
the allocation’s arithmetic return. The Sharpe ratio is a return/

risk measure, where the return (the numerator) is defined as the
incremental annual return of an investment over the risk-free
rate. Risk (the denominator) is defined as the standard deviation
(volatility) of the allocation’s return less the risk-free rate. The risk-
free rate utilized is J.P. Morgan’s long-term assumption for Cash.
Correlation is a statistical measure of the degree to which
the movements of two variables, in this case asset class

returns, are related. Correlation can range from -1 to 1 with
1 indicating that the returns of two assets move directionally
in concert with one another, i.e. they behave in the same
way during the same time. A correlation of 0 indicates
that the returns move independently of each other and
-1 indicates that they move in the opposite direction.

### Page 48

IMPORTANT INFORMATION
Key risks
Alternative investments: Investing in alternative assets involves
higher risks than traditional investments, including, without
limitation, limited liquidity, and is suitable only for investors with
sufficient knowledge and sophistication to evaluate the merits and
risks of such investments. Alternative investments should not be
deemed a complete investment program and distributions are not
guaranteed. They may not be tax efficient, and an investor should

consult with their tax professional prior to investing. Alternative
investments often have higher fees than traditional investments
and they may also be highly leveraged and engage in speculative
investment techniques, which can magnify the investment loss
or gain—including risk of loss of the entire investment. For
comprehensive details around unique set of risks for specific
alternative investments, please consult the offering memorandum.
Bonds: Bonds are subject to interest rate risk, credit and default risk

of the issuer. Bond prices generally fall when interest rates rise.
Commodities: Investments in commodities may have
greater volatility than investments in traditional securities.
The value of commodities may be affected by changes in
overall market movements, commodity index volatility,
changes in interest rates, or factors affecting a particular
industry or commodity, such as drought, floods, weather,
livestock disease, embargoes, tariffs and international

economic, political and regulatory developments. Investing in
commodities creates an opportunity for increased return but,
at the same time, creates the possibility for greater loss.
Diversification: Diversification and asset allocation
does not ensure a profit or protect against loss.
Equities: The price of equity securities may rise or fall due to
changes in the broad market or changes in a company’s financial
condition, sometimes rapidly or unpredictably. Equity securities

are subject to “stock market risk,” meaning that stock prices in
general may decline over short or extended periods of time.
Emerging markets: Investing in emerging markets involves
a greater degree of risk and increased volatility compared
to developed markets. Changes in currency exchange rates
and differences in accounting and taxation policies outside
the investor’s jurisdiction can raise or lower returns. Some
markets may not be as politically and economically stable, and

differences in taxation policies and legal systems outside the
investor’s jurisdiction may create additional risks. Investors
should carefully consider these risks and consult with financial
and legal advisors before investing in emerging markets.
Fixed income: Investing in fixed income products is
subject to certain risks, including interest rate, credit,
inflation, call, prepayment and reinvestment risk.
High yield bonds: High yield bonds are speculative noninvestment

grade bonds that have higher risk of default or other adverse
credit events which are appropriate for high risk investors only.
International investments: International investments may not
be suitable for all investors. International investing involves
a greater degree of risk and increased volatility. Changes
in currency exchange rates and differences in accounting
and taxation policies outside the U.S. can raise or lower
returns. Some overseas markets may not be as politically and

economically stable as the United States and other nations.
Investments in international markets can be more volatile.
Municipal Bonds: Investors should understand the potential
tax liabilities surrounding a municipal bond purchase. Certain
municipal bonds are federally taxed if the holder is subject to
alternative minimum tax. Capital gains, if any, are federally
taxable. The investor should note that the income from
tax-free municipal bond funds may be subject to state and

local taxation and the Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT).
Preferreds: Preferred investments share characteristics of
both stocks and bonds. Preferred securities are typically long-
dated securities with call protection that fall in between debt
and equity in the capital structure. Preferred securities carry
various risks and considerations which include concentration
risk; interest rate risk; lower credit ratings than individual
bonds; a lower claim to assets than a firm’s individual

bonds; higher yields due to these risk characteristics; and
callable implications, meaning the issuing company may
redeem the stock at a certain price after a certain date.
Small capitalization companies: Small capitalization companies
typically carry more risk than well-established blue-chip
companies since smaller companies can carry a higher degree of
market volatility than most large cap and/or blue-chip companies.

TCT Cảng Hàng không Việt Nam (ACV) 
Áp lực lợi nhuận trong chu kỳ mở rộng công suất 
19/11/2026

KQKD 3Q2026 suy giảm do khấu hao 
và biến động tỷ giá

Lượt khách ACV dự kiến tăng chậm 
hơn toàn ngành do tác động từ Phú 
Quốc và Gia Bình

Long Thành dự kiến khai thác từ cuối 
2026, tạo áp lực lên biên lợi nhuận 
giai đoạn đầu

Duy trì khuyến nghị TRUNG LẬP, giá 
mục tiêu 39,500 VND/cổ phiếu

3Q2026, ACV ghi nhận doanh thu đạt 6,066 tỷ VND (-6% yoy) và LNST công ty mẹ 
đạt 2,206 tỷ VND (-14% yoy). Lợi nhuận suy giảm chủ yếu do (1) khấu hao tăng từ 
các nhà ga mới và (2) ghi nhận lỗ tỷ giá 168 tỷ VND trong kỳ, trái ngược với khoản lãi 
tỷ giá cùng kỳ 2025. Lũy kế 9M2026, doanh thu và LNST công ty mẹ lần lượt đạt 
19,226/7,435 tỷ VND (+0%/+2% yoy).

Chúng tôi dự phóng lượt khách quốc tế/nội địa qua hệ thống ACV tăng trưởng bình 
quân 7%/5.5%/năm đến 2030, thấp hơn toàn ngành do (1) sân bay Phú Quốc 
không còn thuộc hệ thống ACV từ đầu 2026 và (2) một phần khách quốc tế tại Nội 
Bài dịch chuyển tự nhiên sang sân bay Gia Bình khi đi vào khai thác. Mức độ dịch 
chuyển trước năm 2030 dự kiến còn hạn chế do phụ thuộc vào tiến độ hoàn thiện 
sân bay và hạ tầng giao thông kết nối.

Cảng HKQT Long Thành giai đoạn 1 dự kiến khai thác một phần từ cuối tháng 
12/2026 và bắt đầu tiếp nhận các chuyến bay quốc tế đường dài từ Tân Sơn Nhất 
vào ngày 28/03/2027, bổ sung công suất 25 triệu khách/năm cho ACV. Tuy nhiên, 
tốc độ chuyển dịch có thể chậm hơn kế hoạch do hạ tầng kết nối chưa hoàn thiện, 
trong khi chi phí khấu hao và vận hành tăng dự kiến gây áp lực lên biên lợi nhuận 
của ACV giai đoạn 2027–2028.

Sử dụng phương pháp chiết khấu dòng tiền, chúng tôi duy trì khuyến nghị TRUNG 
LẬP đối với ACV với giá mục tiêu 39,500 VND/cổ phiếu, tương đương tiềm năng tăng 
giá 4.5% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 18/11/2026.

Cơ cấu doanh thu 2025 Hoạt động kinh doanh

ACV hiện đang quản lý, đầu tư và khai thác 
hệ thống 21 Cảng hàng không trong cả nước, 
bao gồm 10 Cảng hàng không quốc tế và 11

doanh chính của ACV là hoạt động cung cấp 
dịch vụ hỗ trợ trực tiếp cho vận tải hàng

Nguồn: Báo cáo công ty, KBSV

Điểm nhấn đầu tư  Chú thích 
Tăng trưởng bền vững đến từ cả nhóm khách nội địa và quốc tế, tốc độ tăng trung bình dự kiến đạt 
5.5% và 7%/năm giai đoạn 2026-2030. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo 
Long Thành bổ sung 25 triệu khách/năm từ 2027, song khấu hao tăng mạnh khiến lợi nhuận chạm 
đáy năm 2028 trước khi phục hồi từ 2029. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo 
Quá trình lên HOSE của ACV tiến triển thuận lợi nhờ các tài sản nhà nước đang dần được tách bạch, 
tạo thuận lợi cho việc hoàn tất cổ phần hóa tài sản khu bay cho phần vốn nhà nước. 
 Tham khảo trong báo cáo kỳ trước tại đây

HOẠT ĐỘNG KINH DOANH 
KQKD 3Q2026 suy giảm do khấu 
hao và biến động tỷ giá

3Q2026, ACV ghi nhận doanh thu đạt 6,066 tỷ VND (-6% yoy) và LNST công ty 
mẹ đạt 2,206 tỷ VND (-14% yoy). Lợi nhuận suy giảm chủ yếu do (1) khấu hao 
tăng từ các nhà ga mới và (2) ghi nhận lỗ tỷ giá 168 tỷ VND trong kỳ, trái ngược 
với khoản lãi tỷ giá cùng kỳ 2025. Lũy kế 9M2026, doanh thu và LNST công ty 
mẹ lần lượt đạt 19,226/7,435 tỷ VND (+0%/+2% yoy).

Biểu đồ 6. Các quốc gia có lượng khách đến Việt Nam lớn nhất trong 10M2026 (khách) 
Trung Quốc và Hàn Quốc tiếp tục là hai thị 
trường khách quốc tế lớn nhất đến Việt Nam 
trong 10M2026 nhưng tăng trưởng hạn chế 
(+1.4% và -6.3% yoy), chủ yếu chịu tác động 
từ giá nhiên liệu tăng cao, khiến các hãng 
hàng không cắt giảm tần suất bay và du 
khách có xu hướng ưu tiên các điểm đến gần. 
Ngược lại, khách châu Âu tăng mạnh (+51.5% 
yoy), chủ yếu nhờ (1) thị trường Nga phục hồi 
(+150% yoy) sau khi các đường bay thẳng và 
charter được khôi phục, mở rộng và (2) hưởng 
lợi từ chính sách miễn thị thực bổ sung cho 12 
quốc gia châu Âu từ tháng 8/2025 và việc tăng 
cường kết nối hàng không trong năm 2026.

Nguồn: GSO

Triển vọng tăng trưởng hành khách 
tích cực, song ACV dự kiến tăng 
chậm hơn toàn ngành 
 Theo dự báo của Bộ Xây dựng, lượng hành khách thông qua các cảng hàng 
không Việt Nam dự kiến tăng trưởng bình quân 9.7%/năm giai đoạn 2026–
2030, đạt 191.2 triệu lượt vào năm 2030. Về dài hạn, Airbus dự báo lưu lượng 
hành khách hàng không Việt Nam tăng trưởng 7.7%/năm trong 20 năm tới, gần 
gấp khoảng 2 lần mức trung bình toàn cầu (3.9%/năm). Các động lực chính bao 
gồm triển vọng tăng trưởng kinh tế, nhu cầu du lịch gia tăng, chính sách miễn 
thị thực thuận lợi và việc mở rộng mạng đường bay quốc tế, cùng kế hoạch bổ 
sung đội bay của các hãng hàng không.

### Page 5

Chúng tôi dự phóng lượng khách quốc tế và nội địa của ACV tăng trưởng bình 
quân lần lượt 7% và 5.5%/năm đến 2030, thấp hơn mức tăng trưởng chung của 
toàn ngành do (1) sân bay Phú Quốc được chuyển ra khỏi hệ thống ACV từ đầu 
2026 và (2) một phần khách quốc tế tại Nội Bài dịch chuyển tự nhiên sang Gia 
Bình khi sân bay này đi vào khai thác. Chúng tôi đánh giá mức độ dịch chuyển 
trước năm 2030 còn hạn chế do phụ thuộc vào tiến độ xây dựng sân bay và hạ 
tầng kết nối. Bên cạnh đó, đề xuất chuyển các chuyến bay quốc tế từ Nội Bài 
sang Gia Bình hiện chưa được phê duyệt và chưa được phản ánh trong dự 
phóng. Nếu được thông qua, đề xuất này có thể ảnh hưởng đáng kể đến lợi 
nhuận của ACV, do lợi nhuận trên mỗi khách quốc tế cao gấp khoảng 4–5 lần 
khách nội địa, đặc biệt trong giai đoạn sau năm 2030.

Nguồn: ACV, KBSV tổng hợp  Nguồn: ACV, KBSV tổng hợp

Cảng HKQT Long Thành mở ra dư 
địa tăng trưởng dài hạn, song tạo 
áp lực lên biên lợi nhuận trong 
ngắn và trung hạn 
 Cảng HKQT Long Thành dự kiến khai thác một phần từ cuối tháng 12/2026, chủ 
yếu phục vụ các chuyến bay nội địa và một số chuyến quốc tế. Hoạt động 
chuyển các chuyến bay quốc tế đường dài và vận tải hàng hóa từ Tân Sơn Nhất 
dự kiến bắt đầu từ ngày 28/03/2027. Tuy nhiên, chúng tôi đánh giá tốc độ dịch 
chuyển có thể chậm hơn kế hoạch do phụ thuộc vào tiến độ hoàn thiện hạ tầng 
giao thông kết nối với trung tâm TP.HCM, đặc biệt là dự án mở rộng cao tốc 
TP.HCM – Long Thành – Dầu Giây và các dự án kết nối đường sắt bao gồm Thủ 
Thiêm – Long Thành.

Dự án cảng HKQT Long Thành kỳ vọng sẽ mở ra dư địa tăng trưởng dài hạn cho 
ACV, dù gây áp lực lên biên lợi nhuận trong giai đoạn đầu khai thác. Việc đưa 
Long Thành vào vận hành sẽ giúp ACV mở rộng công suất, giảm áp lực quá tải 
tại Tân Sơn Nhất, đồng thời tạo điều kiện thu hút thêm các đường bay quốc tế, 
đặc biệt là đường bay dài và hoạt động vận tải hàng hóa. Tuy nhiên, trong ngắn 
và trung hạn, chi phí khấu hao và vận hành tăng trong khi sản lượng hành 
khách cần thời gian để đạt công suất tối ưu dự kiến sẽ gây áp lực lên biên lợi 
nhuận của ACV giai đoạn 2027–2028. Trong mô hình dự phóng, chúng tôi ghi 
nhận khấu hao Long Thành theo tiến độ nghiệm thu, kết chuyển và đưa vào sử 
dụng từng hạng mục tài sản cố định, thay vì toàn bộ dự án ngay khi bắt đầu vận 
hành.

Nguồn: Báo cáo công ty, KBSV

Chúng tôi khuyến nghị TRUNG LẬP, 
giá mục tiêu 39,500 VND/cổ phiếu  
 Sử dụng phương pháp chiết khấu dòng tiền, chúng tôi duy trì khuyến nghị 
TRUNG LẬP đối với cổ phiếu ACV trong bối cảnh rủi ro từ đề xuất chuyển các 
chuyến bay quốc tế từ Nội Bài sang Gia Bình gia tăng. Giá mục tiêu của ACV là 
39,500 VND/cổ phiếu, tương đương với mức tăng giá tiềm năng 4.5% so với mức 
giá đóng cửa 37,800 VND/cổ phiếu ngày 18/11/2026. Các giả định chính bao 
gồm: 
- Giai đoạn 2026–2030, dự phóng lượt khách quốc tế/nội địa qua hệ 
thống cảng hàng không ACV tăng trưởng bình quân 7%/5.5%/năm, 
thấp hơn tốc độ tăng trưởng chung của ngành hàng không Việt Nam, 
chủ yếu do (1) ACV không còn ghi nhận sản lượng hành khách tại sân 
bay Phú Quốc và (2) giả định một phần khách quốc tế tại Nội Bài dịch 
chuyển tự nhiên sang Gia Bình với mức độ còn hạn chế do hạ tầng kết 
nối chưa hoàn thiện. Dự phóng chưa phản ánh đề xuất chuyển các 
chuyến bay quốc tế sang Gia Bình. 
- Giá dịch vụ trung bình cảng hàng không dự kiến sẽ điều chỉnh tăng 2% 
mỗi 4 năm. 
- Dự án Long Thành giai đoạn 1 và giai đoạn 2 bắt đầu đưa vào khai thác 
từ cuối 2026 và đầu 2032, gia tăng doanh thu nhưng đồng thời tạo áp 
lực lên biên lợi nhuận trong giai đoạn đầu vận hành do chi phí khấu 
hao và vận hành tăng.

### Page 8

Rủi ro giảm đối với giá mục tiêu ACV chủ yếu đến từ khả năng đề xuất chuyển 
các chuyến bay quốc tế từ Nội Bài sang Gia Bình được phê duyệt. Kịch bản cơ 
sở hiện chỉ phản ánh mức dịch chuyển tự nhiên. Trong trường hợp đề xuất 
được thông qua, sản lượng khách quốc tế qua hệ thống ACV có thể thấp hơn 
đáng kể so với dự phóng, kéo theo suy giảm doanh thu và lợi nhuận do khả 
năng sinh lời trên mỗi khách quốc tế cao hơn khoảng 4–5 lần so với khách nội 
địa.

Biểu đồ 13. P/B, ROE lịch sử

P/B của ACV tiếp tục giảm về quanh 1.8–1.9x, 
chạm và có lúc xuống dưới ngưỡng -2std. Đây 
là vùng thấp nhất 5 năm và chiết khấu sâu so 
với mức trung bình 5 năm, phản ánh lo ngại 
về áp lực khấu hao và chi phí khi Long Thành 
đi vào vận hành.

### Page 1

十一月 12, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R   Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新 網通 ‧ 台灣

智邦 
(2345.TW/2345 TT)

AWS、Meta雙引擎加速，2027年維持高速成長 
重要訊息 
智邦公告 3Q26 財務數據，毛利率季減 2.1ppt 至 17.6%，低於預期，主因
零組件漲價，然智邦已逐月調整購料模式，我們認為毛利率谷底已過。 
評論及分析 
3Q26 受零組件漲價影響，毛利率低於預期。3Q26 營收 1,207 億元，季增
26.4%、年增 65.5%，毛利率 17.6%，季減 2.1ppt，低於預期，主因記憶
體等零組件漲價，稅後純益 131 億元，季增 18.3%、年增 67.0%，EPS 
23.39 元。 
4Q26 AI Card 水冷比重增加、調整購料模式，毛利率預估優於 2Q26。我
們認為，4Q26 營收將受惠 AWS AI Card 水冷出貨比重增加、Meta 800GbE 
Switch 持續放量，毛利率則在水冷與 Meta 出貨優化產品組合、調整購料模
式等因素下，毛利率有望優於 2Q26，故我們上修 4Q26 財務預期，EPS 從
23.07 元上修至 28.54 元。 
2027 年三項新規格成新動能看法不變，惟調整 AWS 出貨顆數預期。我們
持續維持智邦 2027 年有 AWS Trainium4 AI C ard、Meta 1.6TbE 交換器、
L11 機櫃組裝業務等三項新規格之看法，惟我們前次報告對於 AWS 2027 年
出貨顆數較為樂觀，我們依據 AWS 資料中心建置進度稍下修 AWS 出貨顆
數，然調升 Trainium3 AI Card 水冷出貨占比，調整 EPS 從 127.22 元至
123.6 元。 
投資建議 
我們維持智邦「增加持股」評等，考量近期台股整體及 AI 供應鏈出現評價
修正，我們將本益比由 25 倍調降至 23 倍，目標價由 3,100 元調降至 2,850
元，係以 2027 年 EPS 123.6 元、23 倍本益比推算。此次調降主要反映評價
調整，非基本面轉弱，我們認為智邦 2027 年營運仍將維持高速成長。 
投資風險 
高階乙太網白牌交換器滲透率不如預期、AI 加速卡市占率流失。 
增加持股‧維持 
  收盤價 November 12 (NT$)  1,525

調整 AWS 出貨顆數預期。

台灣 智邦

財務表現/預估調整 
3Q26 受零組件漲價影響，毛利率低於預期。3Q26 營收 1,207 億元，季增
26.4%、年增 65.5%，毛利率 17.6%，季減 2.1ppt，低於預期，主要是記
憶體等零組件漲價，稅後純益 130.7 億元，季增 18.3%、年增 67.0%，EPS 
23.39 元。 
4Q26 AI Card 水冷比重增加、調整購料模式，毛利率預估優於 2Q26。10
月營收 446.6 億元，月增 10.5%、年增 91.1%，創下歷史新高，主要是
AWS AI Card 水冷出貨比重增加、Meta 800GbE Switch 持續放量，預期
11-12 月營收可望維持成長；毛利率部分，我們預估在水冷與 Meta 出貨量
增加、持續調整購料模式等因素下，有望優於 2Q26；綜合以上，我們上修
4Q26 財務預期，營收由 1,045 億元上修至 1,244 億元，季增 3.0%、年增
72.7%，毛利率由 19.3%上修至 20.1%，稅後純益 160 億元，季增
22.5%、年增 91.6%，EPS 由 23.07 元上修至 28.54 元。 
2026 年四項新規格推升營收年增 6 成。2026 年智邦營收動能來自：(1) 
AWS 400GbE 與 800GbE 交換器、(2) Trainium3 AI Card 放量，且 4Q26 開
始水冷出貨比重增加、(3)Trainium3 機櫃新增 Switch Tray 設計，智邦小量
出貨、(4) 新客戶 Meta 800GbE 交換器放量，最後則是既有客戶 800GbE 交
換器需求增加，故我們調整 2026 年財務預期，營收由 3,892 億元上修至
4,108 億元，年增 65.4%，毛利率由 19.5%調整至 19.2%，稅後純益
484.8 億元，年增 84.0%，EPS 由 84.13 元調整至 86.65 元。 
2027 年三項新規格成新動能看法不變，惟調整 AWS 出貨顆數預期。我們
維持 2027 年 AWS Trainium4 AI Card 與 Switch Tray、Meta 1.6TbE 交換
器、L11 機櫃組裝三項新規格、將成營收新動能之看法，惟我們前次報告對
於 AWS 2027 年出貨顆數較為樂觀，此次依據 AWS 資料中心建置進度，稍
下修 AWS 整體出貨顆數，然調升 Trainium3 AI Card 水冷出貨占比；綜合以
上，我們調整 2027 年財務預期，營收由 5,462 億元調整至 5,393 億元，年
增 31.3%，毛利率略為下調至 19.8%，稅後純益 694 億元，年增 43.1%，
EPS 由 127.22 元 調整至 123.6 元。

ESG圖表定義 
項目 定義 說明 
能源使用 
直接與間接能源消耗總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司運營範圍內消耗之能源總量 
- 能源使用總量 = 直接能源消耗總量 + 間接能源消耗量 
- 總能源使用量包括購買的能源、生產的能源 
- 就公用事業而言，輸電/電網損耗為其業務活動之一部分，被視為總能源消耗，數據不包括
為滿足能源使用而生產的電力(公用事業為出售而生產) 
- 就公用事業而言，用於能源生產的煤、天然氣或核能等原料不屬於「總能源使用」項下

購買之再生能源 
Primary 再生能源購買總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司各種來源之能源消耗量與購買的能源中屬於自然界可再生者(太陽能、風能、水能、生
物質能、地熱能)之量 
- 如無證據顯示再生能源由公司生產，則所報告的能源數據視為購買的再生能源

再生能源使用率 再生能源占總能源消耗量之比例  
CO2 約當排放量 
直接 CO2 與 CO2 約當排放量(公噸) 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量(範圍 1 排放量) 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳(CO2)、甲烷(CH4)、一氧化二氮(N2O)、氫氟碳化物(HFCS)、全氟化合物
(PFCS)、六氟化硫(SF6)、三氟化氮(NF3)

CO2 約當排放量營收比 
直接 CO2 與 CO2 約當排放量(公噸)銷售(百萬元)比 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量(範圍 1 排放量) 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳(CO2)、甲烷(CH4)、一氧化二氮(N2O)、氫氟碳化物(HFCS)、全氟化合物
(PFCS)、六氟化硫(SF6)、三氟化氮(NF3)

廢棄物總量 
廢棄物總量(公噸) 
- 總廢棄物 = 無害廢棄物 + 有害廢棄物 
- 僅考慮固體廢棄物，但如液體廢棄物以公噸為單位呈報，則會將其加入求得包含液體廢棄
物之總量 
- 對於採礦、石油與天然氣等行業，tailings、廢石、煤、飛灰等廢棄物亦考慮在內

廢棄物回收率 
公司呈報的廢棄物回收率 
- 廢棄物回收率 = 廢棄物回收量/總廢棄物*100 
- 廢棄物轉化為能源或經由廢棄物焚燒產生能源視為廢棄物回收 
- 經由堆肥回收的廢棄物視為回收之廢棄物

總取水量 
總取水量(立方公尺) 
- 由呈報組織直接或經水公司等中介機構從任何水源抽取的總水量 
- 水井、城鎮/公用事業/市政用水、河水、地表水等不同之水源均予以考慮

環保支出 
環保支出總金額 
- 所有用於環境保護，或防止、減少、控制環境因素、影響、危害的投資與支出，亦包括處
置、處理、衛生、清理支出

員工流動率 
員工流動率 
- 包括任何原因(自願或非自願)離開公司的員工，如辭職、退休、自然離職/死亡、醫療失
能、冗員、裁員、重組、解僱、裁減或定期合約到期 
- 員工流動率 = (離開之員工/平均員工人數)*100 
- 平均員工人數 = (本年末員工人數 + 去年末員工人數)/2 
- 本財年末員工數 =上個財年末員工數 + 新員工數 - 離開之員工數

女性經理 
女性經理百分比 
- 女性經理占公司經理的百分比 
- 如有不同階層之百分比，如最高階、高階、中階、初階，則會考慮中階女性經理之百分比 
- 女性經理百分比 = 女性經理人數/經理總數*100

女性員工 
女性員工百分比 
- 女性員工占公司員工總數的百分比 
- 女性員工百分比 = 女性人數/員工總數*100

教育訓練總時數 
所有員工教育訓練總時數 
- 僅考慮員工教育訓練時數 
- 包括一般員工所有類型的教育訓練(如健康與安全、環境、急難事件救援、技能與職業發展) 
- 如果數據以天為單位，則乘以 8，係假設 1 天 = 8 小時工作

每位員工教育訓練時數 平均每年每位員工總教育訓練時數  
資料來源：Refinitiv，公司資料

### Page 1

十一月 18, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新 運動用品 ‧美國

Amer Sports 
(AS.O/AS US)

2027 年營運成長展望強勁 
重要訊息 
Amer Sports 3Q26 財報凸顯產品競爭力與品牌溢價。2027 年展望亮眼。 
評論及分析 
3Q26 財報優於預期。3Q26 稅後淨利 2.4 億美元，年增 64%、季 增 124%，
折合每股盈餘 0.41 美元，領先財測之 0.31-0.33、市場預期之 0.35 美元，
主因營收及利潤率皆超前。營收年增 24%、季增 33%至 21.7 億美元，動
能來自 Salomon 與 Arc'teryx 品牌需求暢旺。美洲、歐非中東、大中華區及
亞太平洋銷售分別年增 22%、10%、30%、48%，反映全通路表現亮眼。
加上市場、通路及產品組合有利、促銷折扣減少及費用控管得宜，毛利率
59.3%及營利率 15.8%保持強勁擴張態勢，並優於公司指引及市場共識。 
2027 年初步展望相當穩健。Amer Sports 為目前少數敢對 2027 年提供強勁
展望的品牌商，預 計 營收年增 10-15%與營利率提升約 0.3 個百分點，隱含
獲利將進一步成長近 25%。管理層表示，旗下品牌在北美關鍵城市的知名
度皆在提升，客流量和同店銷售成長趨勢加速；加上進駐更多運動專賣店
或高端運動鞋店，低滲透率預示未來數年成長空間巨大。至於歐洲，儘管
面臨客流量挑戰，滑雪度假村 4Q26 訂單在下雪前即展現高度韌性。 
填補「機能」與「時尚」的真空帶。相較國際競爭對手都在思考如何打造
生活風格類產品，Salomon 憑藉介於奢侈品和戶外運動品牌間獨特的市場
定位，以專業越野性能為骨幹，結合前衛的視覺美學，開創全新的、自主
掌舵的戶外運動鞋細分市場。這精品般的設計感與稀缺性，成功拉高品牌
溢價與定價權，並擄獲年輕及女性消費族群青睞，為推動高速成長之關鍵。 
投資建議 
受惠於 Arc'teryx 及 Salomon 品牌全球版圖快速擴張，我們看好公司營收及
獲利強勁成長動能延伸將有利長期評價向上。我們維持「增加持股」評等，
目標價上修至 47.9 美元，基於 2026-27 年平均每股盈餘預估之 32 倍。 
投資風險 
下檔風險為費不如預期、品牌人氣下滑及經濟衰退。

### Page 2

美國 Amer Sports

十一月 18, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  2

Amer Sports 11 月 17 日法說會重點摘要 
 3Q26 稅後淨利 2.4 億美元，年增 64%、季增 124%，折合每股盈餘
0.41 美元，領先公司財測之 0.31-0.33、市場預期之 0.35 美元，主因
營收及利潤率超前。營收年增 24%、季增 33%至 21.7 億美元，動能
來自 Salomon 與 Arc'teryx 需求暢旺。受惠於市場、通路及產品組合
有利與促銷折扣減少，毛利率擴張至 59.3%，年增 2.5、但季節性季
減 2.4 個百分點 (排除退稅一次性影響)；加上費用控管得宜，營利率
15.8%分別高於公司指引 1.8-2.3、市場共識 1.3 個百分點。 
 市場別營收：美洲 (佔總營收 32%)、歐非中東 (27%)、大中華區 (28%) 
及亞太平洋 (13%) 分別年增 22%、10%、30%、48%，儘管皆較前
一季減速，表現在全球同業中仍格外亮眼。 
 品牌別營收：專業服裝 (包括 Arc'teryx 及 Peak performance，佔總營
收 40%) 及戶外功能 (含 Salomon、Atomic 及 Armada，42%) 分別
年增 26%、27%，反映年輕及女性消費者對新產品反饋極佳。球和
球拍 (18%) 在 Wilson Tennis 360 產品帶動下，年增 12%。 
 通路別營收：直接面對消費者 (佔總營收 45%) 年增高達 36%，遠 超
批發業務 (55%) 之 15%成長。 
 3Q26 庫存週轉天數年增 1%、但季減 31%至 197 天，約當過去三年
同期平均，顯示庫存水準健康。 
 管理層再度上調 2026 年財測展望，現估營收將年增 25-26% (前次為
24%)，符合市場預期。部門別方面，專業服裝、戶外功能、球和球拍
銷售指引皆全面上修，分別年增 27-28%、28-29%、14-15%。加上
營利率估計亦調升至 14.5-14.7% (前次為 14.2-14.5%)，意味著今年
獲利穩健年增 80%無虞。 
 短期而言，預計 4Q26 營收將年增近 20%；但受 3Q26 新增投資遞延
影響，4Q26 營利率將略低於先前預期。 
 2027 年初步指引：營收年增 10-15%與營利率提升 0.3-0.7 個百分點
低緣，暗示獲利將進一步成長近 25%。 
 管理層表示，旗下品牌在北美關鍵城市的知名度皆在提升，客流量和
同店銷售成長趨勢加速；加上與更多運動專賣店或高端運動鞋店建立
合作關係，低滲透率預示未來數年成長空間巨大。 
 歐非中東面臨客流量挑戰，但公司產品卻表現出相當強的韌性。歐洲
滑雪度假村 4Q26 訂單早在下雪前就非常強勁，現在雪才剛開始下，
可能有上修空間。 
 Arc'teryx 在中國位處於獨特的市場地位，介在奢侈品和戶外運動品牌
之間，擁有極具吸引力的價格優勢。隨著新店在各地展開，且同店的
各項關鍵績效指標 (KPI) 也表現良好，管理層有信心成長動能延續。

### Page 3

美國 Amer Sports

十一月 18, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  3

 在運動風格領域，國際競爭同業都在思考如何打造生活風格類產品，
但 Salomon 情況略有不同，而是開創出全新的戶外運動鞋細分市場。
公司將不侷限於產品生命週期管理，更將壯大並激勵消費者持續推動
這個市場規模較小的細分領域進一步發展。 
 門市規劃：(1) Arc'teryx：預計 2026 年淨增加 30-35 間門市，其中約
12 間位於大中華區 (主要集中於 2H26 及 4Q26)，對比目前在北美、
歐非中東及大中華區分別擁有 77、20、144 間門市；(2) Salomon：
預計今年在大中華區淨增加 50 間門市 (前次為 45 間)，對比 3Q26 淨
增加 15 間至 330 間；及 (3) Wilson：預計 2026 年在中國開設 40 家
門市 (包括直營和合作門市，對比 3Q26 淨增加 10 間)。

圖 6: 公司概況  圖 7: Arc'teryx 和 Salomon 為主力品牌 
成立於 1950 年，Amer Sports 為全球知名運動戶外品牌集團，
旗下品牌包括 Arc'teryx、Salomon、Wilson、Peak Performance 
和 Atomic；總部位於芬蘭。2019 年，公司被安踏體育 (中)、

(中) 所組成的財團收購，加快中國業務拓展。2024 年，Amer 
Sports 在紐約證交所掛牌上市。美洲為 2025 年營收最大來源
地區，佔比達 32%，其次分別為大中華區 28%、歐非中東

報酬。 
 持有 (N) 對個股持中性看法，預期個股未來十二個月的表現符合凱基證券集團所追蹤的相關市場的總
報酬。 
 降低持股 (U) 對個股持負面看法，預期個股未來十二個月的表現低於凱基證券集團所追蹤的相關市場的總
報酬。 
 未評等 (NR) 凱基證券未對該個股加以評等。

流，其中包括提供評等給投資人參考。

### Page 1

.
南华宏观热点
——国内经济边际企稳，财政发力是主因，内需仍是短板
翟帅男 （投资咨询证号：Z0013395）
联系邮件：zaisuainan@nawaa.com
投资咨询业务资格：证监许可【2011】1290号
2026年10月20日
..
正文
一、三季度GDP超预期，核心在生产端和外需
前三季度GDP累计同比增长4.6%，其中三季度同比4.5%，高于前值和市场预期。不过，增长成色高度集中在
生产端与外需，并非来自内需。9月规模以上工业增加值同比增长5.5%、环比加快0.3个百分点，1至9月累计
增长5.3%，生产端整体强劲，其中高技术制造和装备制造继续领跑，3D打印设备、锂离子电池、工业机器人
产量分别增长62.5%、39.5%和29.1%。9月工业企业出口交货值名义增长17.0%，生产强与出口强相互印
证。但传统动能仍在拖后腿，水泥、钢材9月产量分别下降5.3%、3.8%，有色金属冶炼增加值下降5.1%，9
月发电量甚至下降1.2%。这说明生产端的景气高度依赖外需与设备更新政策，地产链与部分电子周期下行的

拖累延续，工业的稳定更多是新动能上冲抵消了一部分旧动能拖累。整体看，经济保持“供强需弱、外强内
弱、政策托底”格局，目前处于新旧动能转换期，但内需增长动能仍偏弱。
从两会设立的GDP增长目标来看，四季度只需要达到4.24%的同比增速，全年就可以达到4.5%的增长目标下
限，整体没有压力；而要冲5.0%的目标上限，则需要四季度GDP同比增速达到6.05%，基本不太可能。目前
在外需和新动能的支撑下，GDP完成全年目标下限压力不大，加上而外部美联储紧缩与地缘风险又约束着宽
松的空间，内部化债周期同样造成约束，四季度全面宽松概率不高。

### Page 2

四季度 GDP 推算
资料来源：同花顺  南华研究
二、投资持续负增，消费小幅回暖
投资端持续深度负增长，总量同比下滑7.2%、与1-8月持平。行业上，高技术产业投资增长4.9%，信息传输
业、铁路船舶航空航天、计算机通信设备保持高增，是投资的亮点。制造业投资有所好转，经测算单月同比
跌幅由-6.1%收窄至-0.9%，基建投资同样小幅改善，这是由政策资金落地推动。但房地产开发投资下降
20.6%仍是最大拖累，另外民间投资前三季度下降10.4%，即便扣除房地产后仍下降6.7%，说明民企顺周期
扩张的意愿仍弱，投资主要靠政府和国企在托。
消费小幅上升，略超预期，但回升斜率不高。1-9月社零同比增长1.1%，9月单月同为1.1%，剔除汽车后累计
增速回升至2.8%，可见汽车拖累显著。如果把统计范围扩大到商品加服务，前三季度增长2.6%，服务零售增
长5.0%，服务消费明显强于实物商品。结构上，乡村消费强于城镇，线上服务零售强于线上商品，下沉市场
与体验消费是增量所在。但商品内部分化明显，汽车、建筑装潢、家具、金银珠宝均明显回落，限额以上单

位消费品零售额前三季度整体下降1.5%，耐用品与地产相关消费被收入预期和财富效应压制。展望后市，9
月社零的阶段性回升主要靠基数效应和价格因素，持续性有待观察；四季度能否延续，取决于结构性政策对
于投资和内需的托动作用，以及外需在高基数与贸易摩擦下是否退潮。一旦外需回落，内需又无力承接，届
时政策加码的必要性会进一步上升。

### Page 3

三、外贸强劲，是经济最确定的支撑项
外贸方面，前三季度进出口39.94万亿元、同比增18.8%，9月更是首破5万亿元、增速冲到27.5%，名义规模
与增速都处在高位。出口动能保持强劲，9月出口同比29.6%、前值18.6%，以美元计增速高达36.3%；结构
上看，9月集成电路和汽车出口同比分别增长136%和62%，中国品牌影响力持续提升，家具、家电、服装、
玩具等劳动密集型产品出口增速同样亮眼，说明驱动力正从AI相关链条向传统商品接力。进口同样维持高
增，9月同比24.6%、高于前值21.7%，背后一方面是价格上涨带动，另一方面是电子链条的原材料进口增
加，因此尚不能据此判断内需已经转强。
但要注意，本轮出口的量价结构严重分化，7、8月出口数量增速均为负，出口更多依靠价格支撑；高读数既
受益价格上涨、也夹着抢出口的透支成分，若后续价格回落，数量不能接续，则增速可能快速下滑。综合
看，外贸仍是三季度最稳的支柱，名义高增之下虽有价格与透支的水分、实际量能偏弱，但足以给宏观政策
留出以时间换空间的余地。
